| S148 | Linux v7.2 Documentation/filesystems/proc.rst — https://raw.githubusercontent.com/torvalds/linux/v7.2/Documentation/filesystems/proc.rst | docs | A | The OOM badness heuristic that selects a victim: the 0-1000 range as an approximate proportion of a task's allowed memory, how oom_score_adj is added and its -1000 to +1000 range, the oom_adj legacy scale and the exported oom_score range, for the host kernel 7.2.11 | tag v7.2, Documentation/filesystems/proc.rst sections 3.1 and 3.2, lines 1761-1766, 1777-1783, 1793-1797 and 1811-1812; raw bytes sha256 e760f716f8278deefab17a63739033cadc7faee93663c49e76455afaaf40d126; snapshot .ufo/waves/round-29/w1/ScribeSrcProcRst/S148-proc.rst (2495 lines, 107560 bytes); raw.githubusercontent.com answered HTTP 429 to plain curl on 2026-10-22 so the identical bytes came from git.kernel.org plain at h=v7.2 and cdn.jsdelivr.net gh@v7.2 (cmp clean) | Badness is a score from 0 (never kill) to 1000 (always kill) that is roughly a proportion of the allowed memory a task may allocate; /proc/<pid>/oom_score_adj is added to that score before the kill decision, its acceptable values are -1000 to +1000 where -1000 always reports a badness of 0 and so disables oom killing for the task; the exported /proc/<pid>/oom_score includes the adjustment and is therefore effectively in range [0,2000] | mined | 2026-10-22 | on-release | corpus/knowledge/oom-event-plane.md, corpus/knowledge/kill-event-deep-r8.md, SKILL.md, corpus/lessons/round-29.md |

The suggested backtrace cues are `open_ctree` (indicating the mount stage) and function names containing `replay`, `recover` or `log_tree`; when errors resemble the example, the command should clear the log and the filesystem may mount normally again. Mounted repair most likely causes corruption unless the filesystem is quiescent, which may not be possible to guarantee | mined | 2026-10-22 | on-release | corpus/knowledge/btrfs-repair-r31.md, corpus/knowledge/btrfs-repair-r31.json, SKILL.md, corpus/lessons/round-31.md |
| S167 | btrfs-progs v7.1 Documentation/ch-mount-options.rst — https://raw.githubusercontent.com/kdave/btrfs-progs/v7.1/Documentation/ch-mount-options.rst | docs | A | What btrfs-progs v7.1 documents for the autodefrag, commit= and nodatacow mount options, including their stated defaults, triggers, workloads and consequences; upstream documentation, not host-specific behavior | tag v7.1 (peeled commit 4ab0e80be9e3bb1db2e6038e6d4316d35fb7ba8b), Documentation/ch-mount-options.rst lines 33-51 (autodefrag, workload, latency and reflink warning), 89-101 (periodic transaction commits, default, warning and other commit triggers), 131-159 (nodatacow, nodatasum and consequences); raw bytes sha256 aac3b1b68ba55259f6c2b8e6d1e30e41560eaee3b0f4863af58387271bc9985f; snapshot .ufo/waves/round-31/w1/ScribeSrcMountOpts/ch-mount-options.rst | autodefrag (since 3.0, default off) enables automatic file defragmentation; when enabled, it detects small random writes to files in the tens-of-kilobytes range (currently 64 KiB) and queues them, may not suit large database workloads, may increase read latency while reading adjacent blocks in the affected range, and successive writes merge the blocks in their new location. The warning says defragmenting COW data on Linux kernels <3.9 or ≥3.14-rc2, and stable kernels ≥3.10.31, ≥3.12.12 or ≥3.13.4, breaks reflinks and may considerably increase space use depending on the broken-up reflinks. commit=<seconds> (since 3.12, default 30 seconds) sets the periodic transaction-commit interval while data are synchronized to permanent storage; higher intervals allow more unwritten data to accumulate in memory, with consequences on system crash; the upper bound is not forced, but a warning is printed above 300 seconds (5 minutes). Explicit sync, commands affecting global filesystem state, and internal kernel mechanisms flushing on thresholds or policies (e.g. cgroups) can also commit transactions. The datacow/nodatacow setting defaults to on; nodatacow disables data COW for newly created files, implies nodatasum, disables compression, and gives created files the NOCOW attribute. In-place updates improve performance for frequent-overwrite workloads at the cost of potential partial writes if interrupted by system crash or device failure. Files created under nodatasum have no checksums; turning checksums off gives a slight performance gain because checksum metadata blocks need not be updated, while in-memory checksum computation costs much less than I/O and modern CPUs have hardware checksum support | mined | 2026-10-22 | on-release | corpus/knowledge/btrfs-repair-r31.json, corpus/knowledge/btrfs-repair-r31.md, SKILL.md, corpus/lessons/round-31.md |

If the source is unavailable, or `-r` is set, data is built using RAID redundancy; with `-r`, the source is read only when no other zero-defect mirror exists, an option documented for drives with many read errors and slow access. The source argument can be its device path or filesystem devid; a disconnected source must use devid. Completion removes the source. The target must be the same size as or larger than the source; using all of a larger target requires resizing the filesystem to `<devid>:max` | mined | 2026-10-22 | on-release | corpus/knowledge/btrfs-repair-r31.md, corpus/knowledge/btrfs-repair-r31.json, SKILL.md, corpus/lessons/round-31.md |
| S169 | btrfs-progs v7.1 Documentation/Trim.rst — https://raw.githubusercontent.com/kdave/btrfs-progs/v7.1/Documentation/Trim.rst | docs | A | The three Btrfs trim modes and their invocation, timing, range-size, I/O and performance behavior; manual whole-filesystem fstrim and periodic-service suitability; trim as a device hint and its stated outcomes; documented mkfs and kernel-version defaults; upstream documentation, not host-state evidence | tag v7.1 (peeled commit 4ab0e80be9e3bb1db2e6038e6d4316d35fb7ba8b); Documentation/Trim.rst lines 14-43; raw bytes sha256 2a114d56f29d727002a981e868cc97e89d7dd77a5e1cf42ad5a9ff05bfcf0ffc; snapshot .ufo/waves/round-31/w1/ScribeSrcTrim/Trim.rst (43 lines, 2216 bytes) | Synchronous trim is enabled by mounting with -o discard or -o discard=sync and runs immediately after file extents are freed; it can severely affect performance and is not recommended because ranges may be too fragmented. Asynchronous trim is an improved version of synchronous trim, enabled with -o discard=async; freed extents are first tracked in memory, and trim starts after a period or when enough ranges accumulate, expecting larger ranges and permitting throttling of I/O requests so the rest of filesystem activity is not interfered with. Manual fstrim starts trimming the whole filesystem without mount options; the filesystem traverses free space and starts trim, making this suitable for a periodic service. Trim is a device hint: a device may ignore it, process only ranges meeting criteria, or skip it due to other factors affecting memory cells. Internal data relocation may cause unexpected performance drops, and periodic trim could prevent that too. A trim-capable filesystem created with mkfs.btrfs has trim performed by default on all devices; since kernel 6.2, discard=async is automatically enabled on devices that support it. | mined | 2026-10-22 | on-release | corpus/knowledge/btrfs-repair-r31.json, corpus/knowledge/btrfs-repair-r31.md, SKILL.md, corpus/lessons/round-31.md |

## Paths

- **Rollback net on this host (U1/J4)** — S023 → S018 → S019 → S020 → S028 → S027: begin with Snapper layout and its documented caveats, follow the Arch Limine snapshot integration, then inspect the exact service unit; finish with systemd capability filtering and Linux DAC bypass semantics.
- **Update gate without `-Sy` (U2)** — S010 → S009 → S012 → S001 → S013 → S006 → S005: establish Arch's doctrine first, then the separate temporary-DB check, official-repository metadata, the news feed and date-pinned rollback reference; finish with pacman downloader isolation and the 7.0 local-repository transition.
- **Boot repair on busybox + LUKS + Limine UKI (U3/J3)** — S026 → S014 → S025 → S017 → S015 → S016 → S021 → S022 → S002: begin with boot-stage recovery and flavor-specific initramfs behavior, distinguish systemd breakpoints, establish UKI build routes, then compare v42's changed root path with each busybox hook; finish with the TPM2 notice, applicable only after a systemd-hook and TPM2-policy migration.
- **/boot writers on this host (C2, round 19)** — S066 → S067 → S068 → S073 → S072 → S070 → S071 → S076 → S074 → S075: start from the installed unit, drop-in and hooks, bind each journal event to its unit, then read the plugin and systemd contracts that explain those units, and only then the upstream intent and READMEs.
